G^{h}\equiv\frac{1}{2}(G^{r}+G^{a})=\frac{1}{2}(G^{t}-G^{\bar{t}})\;\;\;\&\;\;\;G^{\cal A}\equiv\frac{1}{2i}(G^{a}-G^{r})=\frac{i}{2}(G^{>}-G^{<}).

(B.5)

The Schwinger-Dyson equations give the full propagator with self-energies resummed; for the CTP contour they are
[
223
,
224
]
:

\displaystyle D(u)G^{ab}(u,v)

\displaystyle=a\delta_{ab}\delta^{4}(u-v)+\sum_{c}c\int d^{4}w\,\Pi^{ac}(u,w)G^{cb}(w,v),

(B.6)

with
a,b,c=\pm
. The differential operator is the Klein-Gordon operator
D(u)=-(\partial^{2}_{u}+M_{(0)}(u)^{2})
for bosons, and the Dirac operator
D(u)=(i\not{\partial}_{u}-M_{(0)}(u))
for fermions. Here we added the subscript
{(0)}
on the mass to indicate these are the tree-level zero-temperature masses. For a system of many fields (flavors), the above expression can be read as a matrix equation in flavor space. The self-energy corrections are denoted by
\Pi^{ab}
. They satisfy the same relations as the Green functions
G^{ab}
, and the advanced/retarded self-energy can be split into real and imaginary parts.
\Pi^{h}
yields a thermal mass correction and
\Pi^{\cal A}
a thermal width.

In a slowly varying bubble wall background, the Schwinger-Dyson equations can be expanded in derivatives. This is most easily done in Wigner space, where the gradient expansion corresponds to an expansion in the diamond operator. To go to Wigner space, introduce the relative and center-of-mass coordinates
r=u-v
and
x=\frac{1}{2}(u+v)
; the Wigner transform is then defined as the Fourier transform with respect to the relative coordinate

G(k,x)=\int{\rm d}^{4}(u-v)\,{\rm e}^{ik.(u-v)}G(u,v)=\int{\rm d}^{4}r\,{\rm e}^{ik.r}G(x+\frac{1}{2}r,x-\frac{1}{2}r).

(B.7)

The Wigner transformed Schwinger-Dyson
eq.

B.6
can be found as follows
[
515
]
. Consider e.g. the position space expression
A(u)B(u,v)
, which can be expanded as

\displaystyle A(u)

\displaystyle=A(x+\frac{1}{2}r)={\rm e}^{\frac{1}{2}r\partial_{x}^{A}}A(x),

\displaystyle B(u,v)

\displaystyle=B(x,r)=\int_{p}{\rm e}^{-ikr}B(x,k).

(B.8)

The Wigner transform is

\displaystyle\int_{r}{\rm e}^{ipr}A(u)B(u,v)

\displaystyle=\int_{r}\int_{k}{\rm e}^{-i(k-p)r}{\rm e}^{\frac{1}{2}r\partial_{x}^{A}}A(x)B(x,k)=\int_{r}\int_{k}{\rm e}^{-i(k-p)r}{\rm e}^{-i\frac{1}{2}\partial^{B}_{k}\partial_{x}^{A}}A(x)B(x,k)

\displaystyle={\rm e}^{-i\frac{1}{2}\partial^{B}_{p}\partial_{x}^{A}}A(x)B(x,p)={\rm e}^{-i\diamond}A(x)B(x,p).

(B.9)

To get the third expression we used integration by parts.
The diamond
operator is defined as

\diamond\big(A(k,x)B(k,x)\big)=\frac{1}{2}\big(\partial_{x}A(k,x)\cdot\partial_{k}B(k,x)-\partial_{k}A(k,x)\cdot\partial_{x}B(k,x)\big).

(B.10)

The resulting set of Wigner-transformed Schwinger-Dyson equations can be split into a hermitian and anti-hermitian part, together known as the Kadanoff-Baym (KB) equations. The constraint equation encodes the spectral information, while the kinetic equation describes the evolution of the system.

In the plasma background the Green’s functions are temperature dependent. In thermal equilibrium they satisfy the KMS relations

iG^{<}(k_{0},\mathbf{k})=s{\rm e}^{\beta k_{0}}iG^{>}(k_{0},\mathbf{k}),

(B.11)

with
\beta=1/T
the inverse temperature and
s=1\,(-1)
for bosons (fermions).

KB equations for bosons

Consider the bosonic system

\mathcal{L}=-\chi_{f}^{\dagger}\left[\partial^{2}+M^{2}_{(0),f}\right]\chi_{f}+\mathcal{L}_{\rm int}(\chi_{f})=-\chi_{m}^{\dagger}\left[\partial^{2}+M_{(0)}^{2}+\Sigma^{2}+2\Sigma\cdot\partial+(\partial\Sigma)\right]\chi_{m}+\mathcal{L}_{\rm int}(\chi_{m}),

(B.12)

where the first expression is in the flavor basis in which the interactions with the thermal bath are diagonal, and the second expression in the mass basis with a diagonal mass matrix
M_{(0)}^{2}
. Here we defined
\Sigma_{\mu}=U^{\dagger}\partial_{\mu}U
with
\chi_{m}=U\chi_{f}
the rotation matrix that diagonalizes the mass matrix. We will give the Kadanoff-Baym (KB) equations in the mass basis.

The constraint and kinetic equation are
21
21

21

The equations are different if one goes first to the mass frame and then performs the Wigner transformation, or whether one first goes to Wigner space and only then transforms to the mass frame. The difference should vanish on the solutions. The diamond operator only acts on
\Sigma
in the
2ik\cdot\Sigma
-term.

(the
[\Sigma,\partial_{x}\Delta^{\lambda}]
-term is dropped in
[
227
]
)

\displaystyle(k^{2}-\frac{1}{4}\partial_{x}^{2})\Delta^{\lambda}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}{\rm e}^{-i\diamond}\bigg(\{M^{2}_{(0)}+\Pi^{h}+\Sigma^{2}-2ik\cdot\Sigma,\Delta^{\lambda}\}+\frac{1}{2}\left([\Sigma,\partial_{x}\Delta^{\lambda}]+\{\Pi^{\lambda},\Delta^{h}\}\right)+\mathcal{C}_{-}\bigg)\,,

(B.13)

\displaystyle 2ik\cdot\partial_{x}\Delta^{\lambda}

\displaystyle={\rm e}^{-i\diamond}\bigg([M^{2}_{(0)}+\Pi^{h}+\Sigma^{2}-2ik\cdot\Sigma(x),\Delta^{\lambda}]+\left(\{\Sigma,\partial\Delta^{\lambda}\}+[\Pi^{\lambda},\Delta^{h}]\right)+\mathcal{C}_{+}\bigg)\,,

(B.14)

with

\mathcal{C}_{\pm}=\frac{1}{2}\left([\Pi^{>},\Delta^{<}]_{\pm}-[\Pi^{<},\Delta^{>}]_{\pm}\right),

(B.15)

and
[..,..]_{-}=[..,..]
and
[..,..]_{+}=\{..,..\}
the commutator and anti-commutator respectively.

The Wightman functions are a function of momentum and collective coordinate
\Delta^{\lambda}=\Delta^{\lambda}(k,x)
,
M_{(0)}^{2}=M_{(0)}^{2}(x)
and
\Sigma=\Sigma(x)
depend on
x
only, and to first approximation
\Pi=\Pi(k)
is the equilibrium space-time independent self-energy. For
n
flavors all these quantities are
n\times n
matrices in flavor space.

\Pi^{h}
gives the thermal mass corrections, which we can simply absorb in the mass matrix
M^{2}=M_{(0)}^{2}+\Pi^{h}
.
The KB equations in the flavor basis follow from
eqs.

B.13
and
B.14
setting
\Sigma=0
and replacing
\{M^{2},\Delta,\Pi\}\to\{M_{f}^{2},\Delta_{f},\Pi_{f}\}
.

The anti-hermitian constraint equation can be solved to find the spectral properties of the system, while the hermitian kinetic equation describes the time evolution. So far, no approximation have been made, and these equations are still exact. The challenge is to find a controlled approximation scheme to describe the plasma dynamics during the electroweak FOPT accurately.
The gradient expansion consists of an expansion of the diamond operator
{\rm e}^{-i\diamond}=1-i\diamond+...
.

For a single free boson – vanishing self-energies
\Pi=0
and constant mass term – the solution for the Wightman functions is:

\displaystyle i\Delta^{>}

\displaystyle=2\pi\delta(k_{0}^{2}-\omega^{2})\,{\rm sgn}(k_{0})\mathfrak{g}^{>}(k_{0},\mathbf{k},x)=2\pi\delta(k_{0}^{2}-\omega^{2})\left[\theta(k_{0})(1+f(k_{0},\mathbf{k},x)+\theta(-k_{0})\bar{f}(k_{0},-\mathbf{k},x)\right],

\displaystyle i\Delta^{<}

\displaystyle=2\pi\delta(k_{0}^{2}-\omega^{2})\,{\rm sgn}(k_{0})\mathfrak{g}^{<}(k_{0},\mathbf{k},x)=2\pi\delta(k_{0}^{2}-\omega^{2})\left[\theta(k_{0})f(k_{0},\mathbf{k},x)+\theta(-k_{0})(1+\bar{f}(k_{0},-\mathbf{k},x))\right],

(B.16)

with

\mathfrak{g}^{>}(k_{0},\mathbf{k},x)=(1+\mathfrak{n}(k_{0},\mathbf{k},x))\;\;\;\&\;\;\;\mathfrak{g}^{<}(k_{0},\mathbf{k},x)=\mathfrak{n}(k_{0},\mathbf{k},x).

(B.17)

To get the rightmost expressions we split into particles
f(k_{0},\mathbf{k},x)=\mathfrak{n}(k_{0},\mathbf{k},x)
with
k_{0}>0
and antiparticles
\bar{f}(k_{0},\mathbf{k},x)=\mathfrak{n}(-k_{0},\mathbf{k},x)
with
k_{0}<0
. For symmetric bubbles
\mathbf{k}\to-\mathbf{k}
is a symmetry.
In thermal equilibrium the Wightman functions satisfy the KMS relation
eq.

B.11
, which implies
\mathfrak{n}_{0}=({\rm e}^{k_{0}/t}-1)^{-1}
the Bose-Einstein distribution.
Here the subscript
0
on the phase space density indicates the equilibrium solution.
The normalization of the spectral functions is fixed by the sum rule

\int\frac{{\rm d}k_{0}}{2\pi}\,k_{0}2\Delta^{\mathcal{A}}=\mathds{1},

(B.18)

which follows from the commutation relations.

The transport equations follow from integrating the kinetic equation over
k_{0}
, using the projections

\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{{\rm d}k_{0}}{2\pi}(2k_{0})(i\Delta^{<})=f(\omega,\mathbf{k},x),\qquad\int^{0}_{-\infty}\frac{{\rm d}k_{0}}{2\pi}(-2k_{0})(i\Delta^{>})=\bar{f}(-\omega,-\mathbf{k},x).\quad

(B.19)

The spatial momentum assignment assures the kinetic
eq.

3.4
in absence of self-energies and gradient corrections gives
u\cdot\partial f(\mathbf{k},x)=u\cdot\partial\bar{f}(\mathbf{k},x)=0
, with
u=(1,\mathbf{k}/\omega)
the 4-velocity.

KB equations for fermions

The Lagrangian for a single Dirac fermion with complex mass term is given in the main text
eq.

3.11
; the corresponding KB equation is
eq.

3.18
.
The fermion propagator is normalized by the sum rule

\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{{\rm d}k_{0}}{2\pi}\frac{1}{4}{\rm Tr}\gamma^{0}2S^{\mathcal{A}}=\mathds{1},

(B.20)

with
S^{-}=S^{>}-S^{<}=-2iS^{\mathcal{A}}
the spectral density.
